Registry RUNNING/PREPARING：

Queued：plan_018_bm25 Phase 2（GPU）、plan_015_dynamic

### 4. 📋 待办（条件触发式）
- M2 Qwen random s42/s43/s44 全完成（已完成）+ embedding/dataflow seeds 完成 → 核实 3-seed R@1 一致性 + 检查 early stopping 行为
- M2 任一 subrun 完成 → cron 回调到来时，独立核实 registry metrics 是否与日志/wandb 一致
- m1_reextract_v2 完成 → 核实 all.jsonl 行数是否达到预期 ~44K，检查 moto timeout 对总量的影响
- plan_005_stratified 转 RUNNING → 核实使用真实 checkpoint 而非 dry-run mock

### 5. 🎯 战略上下文
- **核心结论链**：embedding > random 跨编码器一致 (+1.6-2.0pp, D017)；dataflow 降级为 exploratory (D035)；paper 转型为 systematic analysis + mechanistic insight (D024)
- **DDL**：ARR May 25。M2 ETA ~May 17-18 → post-M2 实验 ~1.7天 → 写作 ~May 20-24

  1. Registry 中 m2_full_starcoder 状态是 DONE(failed) 但实际 StarCoder chain 仍在跑（由 m2_full_starcoder_resumed 接管，PID 519791→519805）
  2. m2_full_qwen 状态是 ANALYZING 但实际 chain 仍在跑（由 m2_full_qwen_continued 接管，PID 337267→122645）
  3. `hard_negative_mining.py` 的 DataFlowAwareMiner 用 embedding distance，但 M2 实际训练数据用 BM25（build_m1_training_data.py）——代码和论文描述已分别修正，不要混淆
  4. Qwen random_s44 best_R1=0.6009 异常高（s42=0.5527, s43=0.5554），M2 完成后需要特别关注这个 outlier seed 是否拉高了 mean

```text
Now I have a much clearer picture. Let me analyze key findings:

## 1. Qwen random_s44 outlier analysis

The training log for random_s44 tells a very clear story:

- **eval@500**: R@1=0.6009, val_loss=0.4252 (best)
- **eval@1000**: R@1=0.5638, val_loss=0.4695 (degraded significantly, patience 1/2)


- **eval@1500**: R@1=0.5592, val_loss=0.4918 (degraded further, patience 2/2 → early stop)
- **Final eval**: R@1=0.5542, val_loss=0.4943

The model peaked at step 500 with R@1=0.6009 and then monotonically declined through the rest of training, eventually dropping to 0.5542 by final evaluation—a loss of 4.67 percentage points. This is textbook overfitting, where the best checkpoint was saved early but the model continued degrading as training progressed.

The real issue is that the 0.6009 value from seed 44 looks like a statistical outlier compared to seeds 42 and 43, which scored 0.5527 and 0.5554 respectively. The validation loss trajectory tells the story: it climbed from 0.4252 to 0.4695 to 0.4918, indicating the model was losing generalization capacity right after that early peak. The high recall at step 500 was likely a lucky artifact of the random seed rather than genuine learning—the model probably just latched onto some early patterns in the data without actually generalizing well.

When I exclude seed 44, the mean drops to 0.5541, but including it inflates the average to 0.5697. This is exactly the kind of scenario the handoff documentation flagged as problematic. The D019 decision threshold relies on bootstrap confidence intervals and paired t-tests, but with only three seeds and this much variance, the confidence interval will be too wide to be meaningful.

I'm also checking the status of the running processes: the StarCoder and one Qwen embedding distance job are both healthy, but there's a duplicate Qwen process that shouldn't be there.

Wait, I'm seeing two Qwen embedding_distance_s43 processes running simultaneously with identical arguments—PID 122645 has been running for 3 hours and is consuming 74.5% CPU, while PID 601229 just started 5 minutes ago and is barely using resources. This looks like an accidental duplicate that got spawned, though it could also be some kind of monitoring subprocess.

The real concern is that both are writing to the same output directory with identical hyperparameters, which means they could be corrupting each other's checkpoints. But given that PID 601229 is only using 1.4% CPU, it might be stuck or waiting rather than actively training—and those other processes with the same uptime could be child processes handling data loading or logging rather than actual training work.

Looking at the VRAM usage is the key here: if there were truly two training processes running, I'd expect to see roughly double the memory consumption, but the GPU is only showing 50401 MiB used, which suggests only one process is actually training.

The second process (PID 601229) is likely stuck in initialization before it's allocated GPU memory, which makes this a serious problem—if it gets past that point, it could cause an OOM error, corrupt the checkpoint directory, or interfere with the active training run.

I'm also noticing something about the watcher setup: the StarCoder watcher is monitoring PID 519791 (the bash chain script parent), not the actual training PID 519805, which is correct since the chain will spawn subsequent subruns under that same parent. But I need to verify how the watcher's process checks are actually working.

Looking at the process list more carefully, I don't see PID 519791 directly listed—it's probably the parent of the timeout wrapper. The watcher has been running for about 10 hours without triggering its kill condition, so 519791 must still be alive, which means the chain is still active.

Now I'm focusing on PID 601229, which started just 5 minutes ago and appears in the embedding_distance_s43 log. This is concerning because there's also PID 122645 that's been running for over 3.5 hours, suggesting duplicate training processes might be running simultaneously. Let me check the Qwen logs to confirm what's happening with these processes. Both processes are running the exact same training script with identical arguments, so this is definitely a duplicate. The low memory usage on PID 601229 suggests it might still be initializing, crashed early, or is a subprocess, so I need to check the process tree and gather more evidence before reporting this issue.

The protocol specifies using R@1 as the primary metric for checkpoint selection, so officially s44's result is 0.6009, but the training dynamics reveal this is likely a noise peak rather than genuine improvement. The three-seed spread of [0.5527, 0.5554, 0.6009] has a standard deviation around 0.027, and the bootstrap confidence interval would be quite wide—probably not statistically significant against baselines.

I'm also noticing a registry staleness issue with the Qwen embedding_distance_s43 checkpoint—the registry shows step 150 from 01:12Z, but the actual logs indicate step 1000, suggesting the metadata hasn't been updated.

StarCoder's random_s43 is progressing normally with step 1850 in the logs and healthy R@1 improvements from 0.4708 to 0.4912.

For Qwen embedding_distance_s43, the eval@500 result shows R@1=0.5446, which is lower than Qwen random_s43's R@1=0.5357 at the same evaluation point, so the embedding distance approach isn't outperforming the baseline yet—though it's too early to draw conclusions without seeing later evaluations.

The duplicate process is the critical issue that needs immediate attention, while the s44 outlier warrants flagging to the Director for statistical planning purposes.
```

</details>

_source line 57 · 2026-05-15T04:15:12.287Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
This resolves the mystery. PID 601229 status shows:

```
Name: pt_data_worker
PPid: 122645
State: S (sleeping)
```

**PID 601229 is a PyTorch DataLoader worker (pt_data_worker), child of PID 122645.** Not a duplicate training process!

`pt_data_worker` is the standard name for PyTorch's multiprocessing DataLoader worker processes. They appear with the same command line as the parent process in `ps` because they're forked from it.

PID 601230 is likely another DataLoader worker, also a child of 122645.


So those processes aren't duplicates at all—they're just DataLoader workers spawned when a new epoch or eval batch started. The fork operation copies the parent's command line, which is why they look identical in `ps`. The low CPU usage makes sense since these workers spend most of their time idle, waiting for the training loop to request more data. They probably came online around step 1000 when the eval phase kicked in.

Now I can focus on the actual issues. Let me look at the Qwen random_s44 outlier that's flagged as a warning.

Looking at the performance metrics, s42 peaked at 0.5527, s43 improved from 0.5357 to 0.5554, but s44 shows 0.6009 as its best—except that peak happened at step 500 and then the model degraded to 0.5542 by the final checkpoint. The 0.6009 looks like a transient spike rather than stable performance. From steps 1000 onward, s44 was actually hovering around 0.555-0.564, which puts it right in line with s42 and s43. That early peak is probably just noise.

For the paper's go/no-go decision using bootstrap confidence intervals and paired t-tests, I need to think about whether using the best R@1 checkpoint values—0.5527, 0.5554, and 0.6009—actually represents what these models can reliably achieve.  The mean would be around 0.5697 with high variance, and with only three seeds the confidence interval will be quite wide. But if I look at stable performance from step 1000 onward, all three converge to roughly 0.55, which is a very different picture. This distinction matters because the main claim hinges on embedding outperforming random by 1.6-2.0 percentage points, and if the random baseline is inflated by s44's outlier, that gap could shrink or lose significance. The protocol requires using best_by_R@1, which locks in the 0.6009 value, but when M2 finishes I should explicitly report s44's training dynamics, consider a sensitivity analysis with and without that seed, and be transparent about whether the generalization advantage claim holds up under scrutiny.

Now looking at the Qwen embedding_distance results: s43 hits 0.5446 at eval@500 compared to random_s43 at 0.5357, a +0.89pp difference that points in the right direction but it's too early to draw conclusions without more evaluation points. Meanwhile StarCoder's random_s43 is showing healthy training dynamics with R@1 improving through step 1500.

Comparing against the previous random_s42 checkpoint, s43 is already outperforming at 0.4912 versus 0.4604. The m1_reextract_v2 extraction is nearly done at 87.1% with four CodeQL workers running, though moto is hitting some timeout issues on one query while others complete fine—expecting to wrap up by mid-May. Both the Qwen and StarCoder registry entries are stale by over 11 hours, which could cause confusion for anyone checking the current status.

The s44 outlier warrants a WARNING rather than just INFO since it directly threatens the statistical integrity of the go/no-go decision, and the director needs to know this immediately to adjust the analysis plan. I should also check whether embedding_distance_s43 has finished its eval@1000 computation yet, since the log I saw ended right at step 1000 with the loss value but the evaluation metrics hadn't appeared.
````

Now I have a complete picture:

1. **Qwen embedding_distance_s43**: Log ends at step 1000, eval@1000 hasn't been logged yet. The training is running step-by-step and will likely eval around now. But wait — the per-subrun log and the s43plus_all.log both end at step 1000. That means the training is at step 1000 now, and the eval at step 1000 is being computed (it takes time to run evaluation on the full val set).

Actually wait, the s43plus_all.log showed content up through embedding_distance_s43 step 1000 as the last entry. 

2. **m1_reextract_v2**: all.jsonl already has 44173 lines, matching the registry count. The extraction has been producing output continuously, though it's only 87.1% complete at 24624 out of 28276 instances. Since the original extraction had 15,897 lines and the reextraction targets 28,276 failed instances, the all.jsonl file appears to be accumulating both the original successful instances and the newly reextracted ones.

3. **StarCoder random_s43**: Now at step 1900, approaching the eval checkpoint at step 2000. Still running on CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1 as confirmed from the chain script output.

Now I'm compiling the report, and the most critical finding is the Qwen random_s44 R@1 outlier, which has significant implications for the go/no-go decision.

Looking at the evidence: s42 peaked at 0.5527, s43 reached 0.5554 with a clear improving trajectory, but s44 spiked to 0.6009 at eval@500 then declined monotonically through subsequent checkpoints. The validation loss tells the story — it degraded from 0.4252 at step 500 onwards, triggering early stopping at step 1500, yet the final R@1 of 0.5542 is essentially equivalent to the other seeds. That initial spike appears to be statistical noise rather than genuine improvement.

Computing the means reveals the issue: using best checkpoints gives 0.5697, but using final checkpoints gives roughly 0.5541 — a 1.6 percentage point gap driven entirely by s44's transient peak. The real question is whether the embedding distance baseline actually outperforms this, which hinges on whether those seeds consistently stay in the 0.54-0.57 range and whether the difference is statistically meaningful.

The D019 protocol locks us into best_by_R@1 scoring, so 0.6009 stands for s44, but that early peak followed by decay is exactly the kind of pattern that raises red flags. Looking at the current runs: Qwen's embedding distance seed is still early at step 1000, StarCoder's random seed is improving steadily toward 0.4912, and there are more experiments queued up.

The m1_reextract_v2 dataset has processed 87.1% of its 44,173 lines and should hit the ETA. Registry metrics for both models are stale by 11+ hours and need refreshing, but the BM25 watchers are active and tracking the right processes. GPU utilization looks healthy across both cards—cuda:0 at 51% and cuda:1 at 57%, both maxed on compute with no idle time, which is expected for the 3B models with LoRA and gradient checkpointing. I'll compile this into a single report with the warnings and status items.

So the log file hasn't been updated in about 15+ minutes, but the GPU is at 100% utilization. This is concerning. Let me think about what could cause this:


The eval@1000 step is probably running through the full validation set of 2980 queries, which could take a while with embedding-based evaluation. But 15+ minutes without any log updates is suspicious, especially since the script uses unbuffered output and tee for logging. The GPU being maxed out rules out a hung process, but something's definitely off with the logging pipeline.

Actually, I'm noticing the Qwen training process is running without the `-u` unbuffered flag, unlike the StarCoder process. That means Python is buffering stdout, and the tee command is also buffering, so the eval results might already be computed but stuck in the buffer waiting to be flushed to disk.

The 100% GPU utilization and 50GB VRAM usage does match what I'd expect during evaluation on the validation set. With about 3000 samples in the validation set and a batch size of 8, that's roughly 375 batches to process. On a 3B model with 2048 token sequences, each batch should take around half a second to a second, putting the total eval time somewhere in the 5-minute range. But it's been about 15 minutes since the log last updated, which suggests either the eval is taking longer than expected or the output is just buffered and hasn't been written yet.

The eval function likely computes both validation loss and recall@k metrics, and the recall computation could be more expensive than I initially thought—it might require encoding all the candidate items in the pool for each query. Regardless, the 100% GPU utilization confirms the process is actively running and not hung. The log will update once the eval finishes and the buffer flushes.

I should check whether the StarCoder log has progressed since it was at step 1900 earlier, and then I can move on to reviewing the swebench worker investigation.

D001\\n- id: D003\\n  date: 2026-05-12 10:02 UTC\\n  summary: 'H2 验证并入 Phase 1: 提取后立即统计 data-flow 连通文件 vs 不连通文件的 gold patch 覆盖率差异'\\n  rationale: Reviewer 指出 H2（特征区分度）在原 MVP 中无独立验证，Phase 2 失败时无法区分是 H2 还是 H3 问题。零成本 sanity\\n    check（统计连通/不连通文件的 gold patch 覆盖率）可在 Phase 1 提取完后立即执行，不需要训练。这比 Phase 2a 的线性分类器更早给出诊断信号。\\n  related_experiments: []\\n  depends_on:\\n  -
